Three-phase Muskat problem: uniform lifespan with respect to the width of the strip between interfaces
Ángel Castro and Liangchen Zou

Abstract
. We consider the three-phase Muskat problem with different densities and the same viscosities. The lifespan of the solutions with respect to the width of the strip between interfaces is studied. Indeed, the interfaces are parameterized by the graph of two functions f ⁡ ( x , t ) f(x,t) and g ⁡ ( x , t ) g(x,t) and we impose that ‖ f ⁡ ( ⋅ , 0 ) − g ⁡ ( ⋅ , 0 ) ‖ L ∞ ≤ C ​ σ ||f(\cdot,0)-g(\cdot,0)||_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\sigma and inf x | f ⁡ ( ⋅ , 0 ) − g ⁡ ( ⋅ , 0 ) | ≥ c ​ σ . \inf_{x}|f(\cdot,0)-g(\cdot,0)|\geq c\sigma. It is shown, under stronger assumption on f ⁡ ( x , 0 ) f(x,0) and g ⁡ ( x , 0 ) g(x,0) , local existence independent of the parameter σ \sigma (with σ \sigma small enough). In order to prove such a result, we need to work in analytic spaces.



1.
Introduction

We consider the Incompressible Porous Media (IPM) system in two-dimensional space, which describes the dynamics of incompressible fluids in a porous media. For simplicity, we take the gravity
g=1
, the permeability of the media
\kappa=1
and the viscosity
\nu=1
so that the fluids satisfy the following equations on
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
t\geq 0
:

(1.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\rho+\nabla\cdot(\rho u)=0,\quad\\
&u+\nabla p+\left(0,\rho\right)=0\\
&\nabla\cdot u=0\end{aligned}\right.,

which take into account mass conservation, Darcy’s law
[
22
]
and incompressibility. Here
\rho=\rho(x,y,t)
is the density,
p=p(x,y,t)
is the pressure, and
u=(u_{1}(x,y,t),u_{2}(x,y,t))
is the velocity field. This system is mathematically equivalent to the motion of an incompressible fluid in a Hele-Shaw cell
[
41
]
.

In particular, we shall study the three-phase Muskat problem
[
38
]
which considers that the density is a piecewise constant function given by

\displaystyle\rho(x,y,t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\rho_{0}&y>\sigma+f(x,t)\\
\rho_{1}&-\sigma+g(x,t)<y<\sigma+f(x,t)\\
\rho_{2}&y<-\sigma+g(x,t)\end{array}\right.

where
\sigma>0
,
\rho_{0}<\rho_{1}<\rho_{2}
and
f
and
g
are smooth functions that decay at infinity and satisfy
2\sigma+f(x,t)-g(x,t)>0
.

From (
1.1
) a system of coupled evolution equations can be obtained for
f
and
g
. Indeed,

(1.2)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f=\frac{\rho_{1}-\rho_{0}}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{y(\partial_{x}f(x)-\partial_{x}f(x-y))}{y^{2}+(f(x,t)-f(x-y,t))^{2}}dy+\frac{\rho_{2}-\rho_{1}}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{y(\partial_{x}g(x)-\partial_{x}g(x-y))}{y^{2}+(2\sigma+f(x)-g(x-y))^{2}}dy,

\displaystyle f(x,0)=f_{0}(x),

(1.3)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}g=\frac{\rho_{2}-\rho_{1}}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{y(\partial_{x}g(x)-\partial_{x}g(x-y))}{y^{2}+(g(x,t)-g(x-y,t))^{2}}dy+\frac{\rho_{1}-\rho_{0}}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{y(\partial_{x}f(x)-\partial_{x}f(x-y))}{y^{2}+(-2\sigma+g(x)-f(x-y))^{2}}dy,

\displaystyle g(x,0)=g_{0}(x).

System (
1.2
)-(
1.3
) was obtained in
[
18
]
, where Córdoba and Gancedo also showed
local existence of solutions in Sobolev spaces
H^{k}(\mathbb{\mathbb{R}})
,
k\geq 4
, and a criterion for the absence of squirt singularities. Another interesting result related to our paper is the criterion for the lack of splash singularities in
[
29
]
. In that paper, Gancedo and Strain prove that there are no splash singularities for the system (
1.2
)-(
1.3
) as far as
\|f\|_{C^{2}}
and
\|g\|_{C^{2}}
remain bounded. Roughly speaking, a splash singularity in this setting is a collision of the interfaces in a point and a squirt (or splat) singularity in a segment.

In this paper we would like to study how system (
1.2
)-(
1.3
) behaves with respect to width between the interfaces
\sigma+f(x,t)
and
-\sigma+g(x,t)
. More specifically, let us assume that
X
is some Banach space to be defined later, that
f_{0}
,
g_{0}\in X
,
f_{0}-g_{0}=\theta_{0}
,
\|\theta_{0}\|_{X}<C\sigma
, for a universal small constant
C
independent of
\sigma
. Also
\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}}(\theta_{0}(x))/\sigma>-1
. In this situation, is the system (
1.2
)-(
1.3
) locally well-posed in
X
with time of existence uniform in
\sigma
? Notice that, if the embedding
\|\cdot\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|\cdot\|_{X}
holds, then

||2\sigma+f_{0}-g_{0}||_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\sigma

and the distance
d_{0}
between the initial interfaces

\displaystyle d_{0}\equiv 2\sigma+\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}}(f_{0}(x)-g_{0}(x))

satisfies

\displaystyle d_{0}\geq\sigma.

Remark 1.1
.

Notice that here we do not ask the interfaces to make flat when
\sigma
goes to zero. Furthermore, although our main result, Theorem
2.2
, needs some smallness condition on
f_{0}
and
g_{0}
, these conditions do not depend on the parameter
\sigma
.

Here it is important to remark that, at least formally, if one takes
\sigma=0
, thus
f=g
, (
1.2
)-(
1.3
) becomes

(1.4)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f=\frac{\rho_{2}-\rho_{0}}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{y(\partial_{x}f(x)-\partial_{x}f(x-y))}{y^{2}+(f(x,t)-f(x-y,t))^{2}}dy,

which is the well-known Muskat equation in the two-phase case.

The main result of this paper is Theorem
2.2
. Next, we present a softer and friendly version:

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
f_{0}
and
g_{0}
be analytic functions in
H^{k}_{\gamma_{0}}
(see Section
2.2
),
\rho_{0}<\rho_{1}<\rho_{2}
,
0<\sigma<1
, and
\mu_{1}
,
\mu_{2}
be given by (
2.8
). Then there exist small constants
\epsilon_{0}
,
\epsilon_{1}>0
, independent of
\sigma
, such that if

\displaystyle\mu_{2}||f_{0}||^{2}_{H^{k}_{\gamma}}+\mu_{1}||g_{0}||^{2}_{H^{k}_{\gamma}}\leq\epsilon_{0}^{2},\quad\text{and}\quad||f_{0}-g_{0}||_{H^{k-3}_{\gamma}}\leq\epsilon_{1}\sigma,

there exists a unique analytic (in space) solution
(f(x,t),g(x,t))
to (
1.2
)-(
1.3
) in an interval
t\in[0,T]
, with
T>0
independent of
\sigma
.

1.1.
A brief summary of previous results on two-phase stable case.

The local existence theory is well-established for equation (
1.4
) with
\rho_{2}>\rho_{0}
(stable case). Here we just review some results in this direction concerning the two-phase stable case, with gravity, the same viscosities and constant permeability and without either surface tension or boundaries. For most of them, one could expect that an analogous version holds for (
1.2
)-(
1.3
) as far as the initial distance between the interfaces is larger than zero.

Córdoba and Gancedo
[
17
]
proved local existence in Sobolev spaces
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}),s\geq 3
. See also
[
7
]
. In
[
15
]
, Constantin, Gancedo, Shvydkoy and Vicol proved local existence for initial data in
W^{2,p}(\mathbb{R})
for
p\in(1,\infty]
. In
[
36
]
, Matioc proved local existence for initial data
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
with
s\in(3/2,2).
In
[
1
]
, Abels and Matioc established local existence for initial data in
W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R})
with
p\in(1,\infty)
and
s\in(1+1/p,2).
Alazard and Lazar established in
[
2
]
local existence for initial data in
\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R})
with
s>3/2
. Same result can be found in
[
39
]
thanks to Nguyen and Pausader. In
[
3
]
Alazard and Nguyen proved local existence for an initial data in the critical space
\dot{W}^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{3/2}(\mathbb{R})
. In
[
4
]
the same authors showed local existence in
H^{3/2+\log}
and, in
[
6
]
, in
H^{3/2}(\mathbb{R})
. In
[
32
]
, García-Juárez, Gómez-Serrano, Nguyen and Pausader proved the existence of self-similar solutions. In
[
31
]
, García-Juárez, Gómez-Serrano, Haziot and Pausader proved local existence when the initial interface has multiple corners and linear growth at infinity. Recently, Sánchez, in
[
42
]
proves the existence of solutions with quadratic growth at the infinity.

The 3D case (two-dimensional interface) has been treated, for example, in
[
17
]
,
[
28
]
,
[
5
]
and
[
39
]
.

The first author together with Córdoba, Fefferman, Gancedo and López-Fernández proved the existence of turning singularities in
[
10
]
and together with Córdoba, Fefferman and Gancedo, breakdown of smoothness in
[
11
]
. Córdoba, Gómez-Serrano and Zlatoš proved in
[
19
]
the existence of solutions that start in the unstable regime, then become stable and finally return to the unstable regime. The same authors in
[
20
]
established the existence of solutions that start in the stable regime, then become unstable and finally return to the stable regime.

There are some results proving global existence for small initial data. It seems doable to extend some of them to the multi-phase case. There are also medium-sized initial data. These ones look, in general, more difficult to adapt to more than one interface. The interested reader could consult, for example,
[
12
,
14
,
13
,
8
,
9
,
21
,
23
]
.

1.2.
Almost-sharp fronts for SQG

A related problem with a similar flavor is the lifespan of almost-sharp fronts for the Surface Quasi-Geostrophic equation (SQG). This equation is also a transport equation for the the temperature
\theta(x,t)
with incompressible velocity
u=R^{\perp}\theta
, where
R_{i}
,
i=1,2
, are the Riesz transform in direction
x_{i}
. If one assumes that the temperature takes only two constant values separated by a curve, an integro-differential equation for the evolution of such curve can be found. In the case, for example, in which,

\displaystyle\theta(x,y,t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\theta_{1}&(x,y)\in\Omega(t),\\
\theta_{2}&(x,y)\in\Omega^{c}(t),\end{array}\right.

where
\Omega(t)
is a bounded and simply connected domain in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, with boundary
\partial\Omega(t)=\{z(\alpha,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\,\,\alpha\in\mathbb{T}\}
, it can be computed that

(1.5)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}z(\alpha,t)=\frac{\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{\partial_{\alpha}z(\alpha,t)-\partial_{\beta}z(\beta,t)}{|z(\alpha,t)-z(\beta,t)|}d\beta.

Expression (
1.5
) is known as the sharp front equation. Local existence has been proven for (
1.5
), in
C^{\infty}

[
40
]
by Rodrigo, in Sobolev spaces
[
30
]
by Gancedo, and in analytic spaces
[
26
]
by Fefferman and Rodrigo.

In this context, an almost-sharp front is a solution of SQG with initial data

\displaystyle\theta(x,y,0)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\theta_{1}&(x,y)\in\Omega^{1},\\
\theta^{m}_{0}&(x,y)\in\Omega^{m},\\
\theta_{2}&(x,y)\in\Omega^{2},\end{array}\right.

where
\theta_{i}
,
i=1,2
, are constants, and
\theta^{m}_{0}
is smooth in
\Omega_{m}
, monotone and such that
\theta(x,y,0)
is smooth in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The domains
\Omega^{1}
,
\Omega^{m}
and
\Omega^{2}
are open, simply connected, disjoint, and
\Omega^{1}\cup\overline{\Omega}^{m}\cup\Omega^{2}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
. In addition, we consider that the domain in the middle,
\Omega^{m}
, has a width of order
\sigma
.

Thus, the question here is whether the almost-sharp fronts are well-posed with existence time independent of
\sigma
. This problem has been treated in the series of papers
[
16
,
25
,
27
]
where the authors studied the asymptotic properties of the equation for the almost-sharp front when
\sigma\to 0
. Finally, in
[
24
]
, Fefferman and Rodrigo were able to prove the existence of families of real-analytic sharp fronts whose lifespan is uniform in
\sigma.
Actually, in these families, the scalar
\theta^{m}(\cdot,\cdot,t)
is who needs to be real-analytic (go to
[
24
]
for a rigorous version of this statement).

Khor and Rodrigo have also considered this problem for a more singular version of SQG (
\alpha
-SQG,
1<\alpha<2
) in
[
34
]
and
[
35
]
.

In our paper, we deal with IPM instead of SQG and in a different scenario since our
\rho_{0}^{m}=\rho_{0}
is constant and
\rho
has two jumps. Furthermore, we try a different strategy that takes advantage of the parabolicity in the Muskat problem, which is not present in (
1.5
). However, in order to make the analysis uniform in
\sigma
, we lose a derivative that forces us to use analytic spaces, as it also happens for the almost-sharp fronts in SQG.

1.3.
Organization of the paper.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
we present the functional setting in which we will solve the equations and Theorem
2.2
which is the main result of this paper. The proof of Theorem
2.2
runs along Sections
3
,
4
and
5
.

Section
3
contains the a priori energy estimates. We have decided to present all the details in the computations and pay the price of a long extension. Let us sketch it to emphasize what the important points are:

The most important sections are
3.2
and
3.4
. In
3.2
we deal with the higher order terms in derivatives in the equation, that is, the most singular terms. In order to estimate them, we just use parabolicity, and then we could produce similar estimates in classical Sobolev spaces
H^{k}(\mathbb{R})
. In Section
3.4
, we deal with the terms involving
(P_{12}-P_{21})
in (
3.9
)-(
3.12
), or equivalently, the left-hand side of (
3.58
)-(
3.59
). In these terms, we lose a derivative that cannot be canceled out by the parabolicity uniformly in
\sigma
. This is the only term we would not be able to handle in the case we work in classical Sobolev spaces. Thus, we are forced to introduce analytic spaces
H^{k}_{\gamma}
just because of the terms involving
(P_{12}-P_{21})
in (
3.9
)-(
3.12
).

In Sections
3.3
,
3.5
,
3.6
,
3.7
,
3.8
and
3.9
we deal with lower-order terms. These sections could be avoided in a first reading since they are rather technical.

In Section
4
we control the distance between the interface by using Section
3
. We need this control to close the proof of Theorem
2.2
.

Finally, in Section
5
we show how estimates in Section
3
and
4
yield the proof of Theorem
2.2
.

2.
Functional setting and main Theorem

For the sake of completeness and to make clear our notation, next we give a derivation of the equations (
1.2
)-(
1.3
) in the three-phase case. As we said before, a similar exposition (slightly different) can be found in
[
18
]
. After that we will present the functional setting and the main result of this paper.

2.1.
Equations for multi-phase Muskat problem.

We suppose that
\rho
is given by a profile function
\overline{\rho}(y)
and the level set function
\eta=\eta(x,y,t)
in the sense that

(2.1)

\rho(x,y+\eta(x,y,t),t)=\overline{\rho}(y)

for
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
t\geq 0
.
Taking derivatives in (
2.1
) yields

(2.2)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho+\partial_{y}\rho\partial_{t}\eta

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\rho+\partial_{y}\rho\partial_{x}\eta

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{y}\rho\left(1+\partial_{y}\eta\right)

\displaystyle=\partial_{y}\overline{\rho}.

Then we derive the equation satisfied by
\eta
from (
2.2
) and (
1.1
):

(2.3)

\partial_{t}\eta+u_{1}\partial_{x}\eta-u_{2}=0.

Meanwhile, assuming the hydrostatic condition for the pressure,
u
can be expressed in terms of
\rho
through the Riesz transform:

u_{1}(x,y,t)=-\left(-\Delta\right)^{-1}\partial_{y}\partial_{x}\rho=\frac{1}{2\pi}P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\alpha\partial_{y}\rho\left(x-\alpha,\overline{y},t\right)}{\alpha^{2}+\left(y-\overline{y}\right)^{2}}d\alpha d\overline{y},

u_{2}(x,y,t)=\left(-\Delta\right)^{-1}\partial_{x}^{2}\rho=-\frac{1}{2\pi}P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\alpha\partial_{x}\rho\left(x-\alpha,\overline{y},t\right)}{\alpha^{2}+\left(y-\overline{y}\right)^{2}}d\alpha d\overline{y}.

Replacing
y
by
y+\eta(x,y,t)
and applying the change of variable
\left(\alpha,\overline{y}\right):=\left(\alpha,y_{1}+\eta(x-\alpha,y_{1},t)\right)
in the above two equations yields

(2.4)

u_{1}(x,y+\eta,t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\alpha\partial_{y}\overline{\rho}(y_{1})}{\alpha^{2}+\left(y-y_{1}+\eta(x,y,t)-\eta(x-\alpha,y_{1},t)\right)^{2}}d\alpha dy_{1},

(2.5)

u_{2}(x,y+\eta,t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\alpha\partial_{x}\eta(x-\alpha,y_{1},t)\partial_{y}\overline{\rho}(y_{1})}{\alpha^{2}+\left(y-y_{1}+\eta(x,y,t)-\eta(x-\alpha,y_{1},t)\right)^{2}}d\alpha dy_{1}.

Then expressing
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
by (
2.4
-
2.5
) in (
2.3
) gives

(2.6)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\eta(x,y,t)=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\pi}P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\alpha\left(\partial_{x}\eta(x,y,t)-\partial_{x}\eta(x-\alpha,y_{1},t)\right)\partial_{y}\overline{\rho}(y_{1})}{\alpha^{2}+\left(y-y_{1}+\eta(x,y,t)-\eta(x-\alpha,y_{1},t)\right)^{2}}d\alpha dy_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-u_{1}(x,y+\eta,t)\partial_{x}\eta(x,y,t)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2\pi}P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\alpha\cdot\partial_{x}\eta(x-\alpha,y_{1},t)\partial_{y}\overline{\rho}(y_{1})}{\alpha^{2}+\left(y-y_{1}+\eta(x,y,t)-\eta(x-\alpha,y_{1},t)\right)^{2}}d\alpha dy_{1}.

We consider the case in which the profile
\overline{\rho}
is a step function:

(2.7)

\overline{\rho}(y)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\rho_{0},\quad y>\sigma,\\
&\rho_{1},\quad\sigma>y>-\sigma,\\
&\rho_{2},\quad-\sigma>y,\end{aligned}\right.

where
\sigma>0
is a given constant and
\rho_{0}<\rho_{1}<\rho_{2}
are real constants. In this case, the derivative
\partial_{y}\overline{\rho}
is the sum of two dirac functions:

\partial_{y}\overline{\rho}=-2\pi\Delta\rho\left(\mu_{2}\delta(y-\sigma)+\mu_{1}\delta(y+\sigma)\right),

where we denote

(2.8)

\Delta\rho:=\frac{\rho_{2}-\rho_{0}}{2\pi},\;\mu_{1}:=(\rho_{2}-\rho_{0})^{-1}\left(\rho_{2}-\rho_{1}\right),\;\mu_{2}:=(\rho_{2}-\rho_{0})^{-1}\left(\rho_{1}-\rho_{0}\right).

Note that
\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}=1
. Now let

f(x,t):=\eta(x,\sigma,t),\quad g(x,t):=\eta(x,-\sigma,t),

u_{+}(x,t):=u_{1}(x,\sigma+\eta(x,\sigma,t),t),\quad u_{-}(x,t):=u_{1}(x,-\sigma+\eta(x,-\sigma,t),t).

From (
2.4
-
2.6
), it follows

(2.9)

\displaystyle u_{+}(x,t)=

\displaystyle-\mu_{2}\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{2}+\left(\Delta f(x,x-\alpha,t)\right)^{2}}d\alpha

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{2}+\left(2\sigma+f(x,t)-g(x-\alpha,t)\right)^{2}}d\alpha,

(2.10)

\displaystyle u_{-}(x,t)=

\displaystyle-\mu_{2}\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{2}+\left(-2\sigma+g(x,t)-f(x-\alpha,t)\right)^{2}}d\alpha

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{2}+\left(\Delta g(x,x-\alpha,t)\right)^{2}}d\alpha,

(2.11)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f+u_{+}\partial_{x}f=

\displaystyle-\mu_{2}\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\alpha\cdot\partial_{x}f(x-\alpha,t)}{\alpha^{2}+\left(\Delta f(x,x-\alpha,t)\right)^{2}}d\alpha

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\alpha\cdot\partial_{x}g(x-\alpha,t)}{\alpha^{2}+\left(2\sigma+f(x,t)-g(x-\alpha,t)\right)^{2}}d\alpha,

(2.12)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}g+u_{-}\partial_{x}g=

\displaystyle-\mu_{2}\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\alpha\cdot\partial_{x}f(x-\alpha,t)}{\alpha^{2}+\left(-2\sigma+g(x,t)-f(x-\alpha,t)\right)^{2}}d\alpha

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\alpha\cdot\partial_{x}g(x-\alpha,t)}{\alpha^{2}+\left(\Delta g(x,x-\alpha,t)\right)^{2}}d\alpha.

Here we have introduced the notation

(2.13)

\Delta w(x,x_{1},t):=w(x,t)-w(x_{1},t)

for arbitrary function
w
. In particular,
\Delta x=x-x_{1}
. Along the paper we will also use

\displaystyle\tilde{\Delta}w(x,\alpha,t):=\Delta w(x,x-\alpha,t)=w(x,t)-w(x-\alpha,t).

In most of the paper we will not make explicit the dependence on time. When no confusion is possible, we will also omit the dependence on the spatial variables and just write either
\Delta w
or
\tilde{\Delta}w
.

For simplicity, we write

(2.14)

\displaystyle P_{11}(x,x-\alpha):=\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{2}+(\Delta f(x,x-\alpha))^{2}},\quad P_{12}(x,x-\alpha):=\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{2}+(2\sigma+f(x,t)-g(x-\alpha))^{2}},

\displaystyle P_{21}(x,x-\alpha):=\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{2}+(2\sigma+f(x-\alpha)-g(x))^{2}},\quad P_{22}(x,x-\alpha):=\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{2}+(\Delta g(x,x-\alpha)})^{2},

and subsequently

u_{+}(x,t)=-\mu_{2}\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}P_{11}(x,x-\alpha)d\alpha-\mu_{1}\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}P_{12}(x,x-\alpha)d\alpha,

u_{-}(x,t)=-\mu_{2}\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}P_{21}(x,x-\alpha)d\alpha-\mu_{1}\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}P_{22}(x,x-\alpha)d\alpha.

To apply a further transformation, we set

h:=\mu_{2}f+\mu_{1}g,\quad\theta:=f-g.

Then we deduce the equations satisfied by
h
and
\theta
from (
2.11
-
2.12
):

(2.15)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}h+\left(\mu_{2}u_{+}+\mu_{1}u_{-}\right)\partial_{x}h+\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\left(u_{+}-u_{-}\right)\partial_{x}\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\mu_{2}^{2}P_{11}+\mu_{1}\mu_{2}P_{12}+\mu_{1}\mu_{2}P_{21}+\mu_{1}^{2}P_{22}\right)(x,x-\alpha)\partial_{x}h(x-\alpha)d\alpha

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\mu_{2}(P_{11}-P_{12})-\mu_{1}(P_{22}-P_{21})\right)(x,x-\alpha)\partial_{x}\theta(x-\alpha)d\alpha,

(2.16)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\theta+\left(u_{+}-u_{-}\right)\partial_{x}h+\left(\mu_{1}u_{+}+\mu_{2}u_{-}\right)\partial_{x}\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\mu_{2}(P_{11}-P_{21})-\mu_{1}(P_{22}-P_{12})\right)(x,x-\alpha)\partial_{x}h(x-\alpha)d\alpha

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(P_{11}+P_{22}-P_{12}-P_{21}\right)(x,x-\alpha)\partial_{x}\theta(x-\alpha)d\alpha.

2.2.
Preliminaries

We will consider the functional space that consists of functions which can be extended analytically to a strip of width
\gamma
on the complex plane
\Omega_{\gamma}:=\left\{x\in\mathbb{C}\mid|\Im x|<\gamma\right\},
and whose restriction to the boundary of
\Omega_{\gamma}
belongs to
L^{p}
. We equipped this space with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}_{\gamma}}
defined by

\|f\|^{p}_{L^{p}_{\gamma}}:=\|f(\cdot+i\gamma)\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}+\|f(\cdot-i\gamma)\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}.

Specifically, we denote this functional space by

L^{p}_{\gamma}:=\left\{f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R})\mid f\text{ can be extended analytically to }\Omega_{\gamma},\;\|f\|_{L^{p}_{\gamma}}<+\infty\right\}.

We also denote the corresponding Sobolev space by

H^{k}_{\gamma}:=\left\{f\in L^{2}_{\gamma}\mid\partial_{x}^{j}f\in L^{2}_{\gamma}\text{ for each }j\in[0,k]\cap\mathbb{Z}\right\}

with the norm defined by

\|f\|_{H^{k}_{\gamma}}^{2}:=\|f(\cdot+i\gamma)\|_{H^{k}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\|f(\cdot-i\gamma)\|_{H^{k}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}.

For later use, we define the operator
\Lambda
as
\hat{\Lambda f}(\xi)=|\xi|\hat{f}(\xi)
, where
\hat{f}
denotes the Fourier transform of
f
. An important inequality in our analysis will be the following.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
f(\cdot,t)\in C^{1}\left([0,T];H^{k}_{\gamma(t)}\right)
with
k\geq 1
and
\gamma(t)
be a positive time-dependent decreasing
C^{1}
function. Then

(2.17)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma(t)}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\Re\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}f(x+i\gamma(t),t)\overline{f(x+i\gamma(t),t)}dx+2\Re\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}f(x-i\gamma(t),t)\overline{f(x-i\gamma(t),t)}dx

\displaystyle+2\gamma^{\prime}(t)\tanh{(2\gamma(t))}\|\Lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}f\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma(t)}}^{2}-2\gamma^{\prime}(t)\tanh{(2\gamma(t))}\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma(t)}}^{2}.

Proof.

For
f(\cdot,t)\in C^{1}\left([0,T];H^{k}_{\gamma(t)}\right)
and a time-dependent function
\gamma(t)
, there is

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma(t)}}^{2}=

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left(\|f(\cdot+i\gamma(t),t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\|f(\cdot-i\gamma(t),t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\hat{f}(\xi,t)|^{2}(e^{2\gamma(t)\xi}+e^{-2\gamma(t)\xi})d\xi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\Re\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}\hat{f}(\xi,t)\overline{\hat{f}(\xi,t)}(e^{2\gamma(t)\xi}+e^{-2\gamma(t)\xi})d\xi

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\pi}\gamma^{\prime}(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\hat{f}(\xi,t)|^{2}|\xi|(e^{2\gamma(t)|\xi|}-e^{-2\gamma(t)|\xi|})d\xi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\Re\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}f(x+i\gamma(t),t)\overline{f(x+i\gamma(t),t)}dx+2\Re\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}f(x-i\gamma(t),t)\overline{f(x-i\gamma(t),t)}dx

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{\pi}\gamma^{\prime}(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\hat{f}(\xi,t)|^{2}|\xi|\sinh{(2\gamma(t)|\xi|)}d\xi.

Note that

\displaystyle|\xi|\sinh{(2\gamma(t)|\xi|)}

\displaystyle=|\xi|\cosh{(2\gamma(t)|\xi|)\tanh{(2\gamma(t)|\xi|)}}

\displaystyle\geq|\xi|\cosh{(2\gamma(t)|\xi|)}\tanh{(2\gamma(t))}-\cosh{(2\gamma(t)|\xi|)}\tanh{(2\gamma(t))}.

Hence for decreasing
\gamma(t)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma(t)}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\Re\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}f(x+i\gamma(t),t)\overline{f(x+i\gamma(t),t)}dx+2\Re\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}f(x-i\gamma(t),t)\overline{f(x-i\gamma(t),t)}dx

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{\pi}\gamma^{\prime}(t)\tanh{(2\gamma(t))}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\hat{f}(\xi,t)|^{2}|\xi|\cosh{(2\gamma(t)\xi)}d\xi

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{\pi}\gamma^{\prime}(t)\tanh{(2\gamma(t))}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\hat{f}(\xi,t)|^{2}\cosh{(2\gamma(t)\xi)}d\xi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\Re\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}f(x+i\gamma(t),t)\overline{f(x+i\gamma(t),t)}dx+2\Re\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}f(x-i\gamma(t),t)\overline{f(x-i\gamma(t),t)}dx

\displaystyle+2\gamma^{\prime}(t)\tanh{(2\gamma(t))}\|\Lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}f\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma(t)}}^{2}-2\gamma^{\prime}(t)\tanh{(2\gamma(t))}\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma(t)}}^{2}.

∎

2.3.
Statement of the main result

In this paper, we consider the initial
data
(h_{0},\theta_{0})\in H^{k}_{\gamma_{0}}
for
k\geq 10
and
\gamma_{0}>0

with the control of norms

(2.18)

\|h_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{k}_{\gamma_{0}}}+\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\|\theta_{0}\|^{2}_{H^{k}_{\gamma_{0}}}\leq\epsilon^{2}_{0}.

Here
\mu_{1}
,
\mu_{2}
are given by (
2.8
), and
\epsilon_{0}
is a small constant independent on
\sigma
which will be determined later.
Moreover, we suppose that the initial distance between the two interfaces is proportional to
\sigma
:

(2.19)

\|\theta_{0}\|_{H^{k-3}_{\gamma_{0}}}\leq\sigma\epsilon_{1}

for some small constant
\epsilon_{1}
independent of
\sigma
.
The main result is the uniform local existence of solutions to (
2.15
-
2.16
) as
\sigma
tends to
0
with initial data satisfying (
2.18
-
2.19
). Since we are only concerned with small
\sigma
, assume that
\sigma<1
without loss of generality.

Theorem 2.2
.

Suppose that
\sigma<1
. Let
h_{0}
and
\theta_{0}
be analytic functions that belong to
H_{\gamma_{0}}^{k}
with
k\geq 10
and satisfy (
2.18
-
2.19
) for small constants
\epsilon_{0}
,
\epsilon_{1}
and a given constant
\gamma_{0}>0
. Then there exists
T>0
independent of
\sigma
and a unique solution
(h(t),\theta(t))
to (
2.15
-
2.16
) on
[0,T]
satisfying

(2.20)

\|h(t)\|^{2}_{H^{k}_{\gamma(t)}}+\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\|\theta(t)\|^{2}_{H^{k}_{\gamma(t)}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}^{2},

(2.21)

\|\theta(t)\|^{2}_{H^{k-3}_{\gamma(t)}}\lesssim\sigma(\epsilon_{1}^{2}+\epsilon_{0}^{2}).

Here
\mu_{1}
,
\mu_{2}
are given by (
2.8
), and
\gamma(t)
is a time-dependent positive width function satisfying
\gamma(0)=\gamma_{0}
and

(2.22)

2\gamma^{\prime}(t)\tanh(2\gamma(t))=-C_{2}\left(\|\partial_{x}h(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{\gamma(t)}}+\|\partial_{x}\theta(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{\gamma(t)}}\right),

where
C_{2}
is a universal constant.

Remark 2.3

(Parabolicity and choice of the unknown)
.

We notice that, when linearized at
f=g=0
, the equation (
2.11
) and (
2.12
) becomes

\partial_{t}f=-\mu_{2}\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\partial_{x}f(x-\alpha,t)}{\alpha}d\alpha-\mu_{1}\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\alpha\cdot\partial_{x}g(x-\alpha),t)}{\alpha^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}}d\alpha,

\partial_{t}g=-\mu_{2}\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\alpha\cdot\partial_{x}f(x-\alpha,t)}{\alpha^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}}d\alpha-\mu_{1}\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\partial_{x}g(x-\alpha,t)}{\alpha}d\alpha.

If one further takes
\sigma=0
, the right-hand sides of the above two equations become the same:

-\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{(\mu_{2}\partial_{x}f+\mu_{1}\partial_{x}g)(x-\alpha,t)}{\alpha}d\alpha=-\Delta\rho\Lambda h.

Consequently, we get a first order parabolic equation of
h
:

\partial_{t}h=-\Delta\rho\Lambda h.
Therefore, one can expect to gain half a derivative in
h
in the energy estimate. Moreover, by multiplying linearized (
2.11
) and (
2.12
) by
\mu_{2}\overline{f}
and
\mu_{1}\overline{g}
respectively, integrating over
x\in\Gamma_{\pm}(t)
and taking the real parts, the left-hand sides will give rise to

(2.23)

\displaystyle\mu_{2}\Re\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}f(x+i\gamma(t),t)\overline{f(x+i\gamma(t),t)}dx+\mu_{2}\Re\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}f(x-i\gamma(t),t)\overline{f(x-i\gamma(t),t)}dx

\displaystyle+\mu_{1}\Re\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}g(x+i\gamma(t),t)\overline{g(x+i\gamma(t),t)}dx+\mu_{1}\Re\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}g(x-i\gamma(t),t)\overline{g(x-i\gamma(t),t)}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}\left(\mu_{2}\|f\|_{H^{k}_{\gamma(t)}}^{2}+\mu_{1}\|g\|_{H^{k}_{\gamma(t)}}^{2}\right)-\frac{\mu_{2}}{\pi}\gamma^{\prime}(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\hat{f}(\xi,t)|^{2}|\xi|\sinh\left(2\gamma(t)|\xi|\right)d\xi

\displaystyle-\frac{\mu_{1}}{\pi}\gamma^{\prime}(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\hat{g}(\xi,t)|^{2}|\xi|\sinh\left(2\gamma(t)|\xi|\right)d\xi,

and the terms on the right-hand sides will give rise to

\displaystyle-\mu_{2}^{2}\,\Re P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\frac{\partial_{x}f(x-\alpha)\overline{f(x)}}{\alpha}d\alpha dx-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\,\Re P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\frac{\alpha\cdot\partial_{x}g(x-\alpha)\overline{f(x)}}{\alpha^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}}d\alpha dx,

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\Re\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\frac{\alpha\partial_{x}f(x-\alpha)\overline{g(x)}}{\alpha^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}}d\alpha dx-\mu_{1}^{2}\Re\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\frac{\partial_{x}g(x-\alpha)\overline{g(x)}}{\alpha}d\alpha dx.

Here we dropped the common coefficient
\Delta\rho
and the varriable
t
for simplicity. Using the identity
\partial_{x}f(x-\alpha)=-\partial_{\alpha}\left(f(x-\alpha)-f(x)\right)
and the symmetry between
x
and
x-\alpha
, after integrating by parts in
\alpha
, the sum of the above terms becomes

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm(t)}}\left(\mu_{2}^{2}\frac{\left|\Delta f(x,x-\alpha)\right|^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}+\mu_{1}^{2}\frac{\left|\Delta g(x,x-\alpha)\right|^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\right.

\displaystyle+\left.2\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\frac{\alpha^{2}-(2\sigma)^{2}}{\left(\alpha^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}\right)^{2}}\Re\left(\Delta f(x,x-\alpha)\cdot\overline{\Delta g(x,x-\alpha)}\right)\right)d\alpha dx,

which will be a dissipation term in the energy estimate.
If one takes
\sigma=0
, the integrand is exactly
\alpha^{-2}\left|\mu_{2}\Delta f+\mu_{1}\Delta g\right|^{2}=\alpha^{-2}\left|\Delta h\right|^{2}
. Since we are concerned with small
\sigma>0
, in general cases, the integrand can be viewed as a perturbation of
\alpha^{-2}\left|\Delta h\right|^{2}
. In fact, by setting
\theta=f-g
, it can be shown that

\mu_{2}^{2}\frac{\left|\Delta f\right|^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}+\mu_{1}^{2}\frac{\left|\Delta g\right|^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}+2\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\frac{\alpha^{2}-(2\sigma)^{2}}{\left(\alpha^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}\right)^{2}}\Re\left(\Delta f\cdot\overline{\Delta g}\right)\simeq\frac{|\Delta h|^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}+\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}^{2}\frac{(2\sigma)^{2}}{\alpha^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}}\frac{|\Delta\theta|^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}.

One reason to introduce
h
and
\theta
is that they are the eigenfunctions in the previous diagonalization. Indeed, the eigenvalue corresponding to
\Delta h
has lower and upper bounds independent of
\sigma
, and the eigenvalue corresponding to
\theta
depends on
\sigma
. While the above quadratic form is positive-definite, the factor
\frac{(2\sigma)^{2}}{\alpha^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}}
vanishes ununiformly in
\alpha
as
\sigma
goes to
0
. Moreover, the first term on the right-hand side of (
2.23
) can be expressed in terms of
h
and
\theta
:

\mu_{2}\|f\|_{H^{k}_{\gamma(t)}}^{2}+\mu_{1}\|g\|_{H^{k}_{\gamma(t)}}^{2}=\|h\|_{H^{k}_{\gamma(t)}}^{2}+\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\|\theta\|_{H^{k}_{\gamma(t)}}^{2},

which are regarded as higher order energy throughout the paper. The other reason is that in order to bound the nonlinear terms, we will have to control
f-g
and show that
\sigma^{-1}\|f-g\|_{H^{k-3}_{\gamma(t)}}\leq C
with a constant
C
independent of
\sigma
.

Remark 2.4
.

A very important remark for this paper is the following: in order to prove Theorem
2.2
we will use energy estimates. Thus, we will have to fight against the loss of derivatives which appears in many terms throughout the analysis. Actually, to handle all these terms, one can simply use the gain of derivatives from losing analyticity by choosing
-\gamma^{\prime}
large enough in Lemma
2.17
. For example, the integral of the quadratic form (
3.15
) can be absorbed regardless of its sign if we choose
-\gamma^{\prime}>C
for certain large constant
C
. However, in order to avoid losing analyticity and make
-\gamma^{\prime}
as small as possible, we will use the parabolicity in (
3.15
) instead of losing analyticity whenever it is possible to do so. Indeed, the only essential terms that cannot be absorbed by the parabolicity in (
3.15
) are the anti-symmetric terms involving
(P_{12}-P_{21})
in (
3.9
-
3.12
). These terms force us to make use of the analytic functional space and handle them by losing analyticity. Subsequently, we will also use the loss of analyticity to handle the transport terms, since the velocities are then not real-valued, and we are no longer able to deal with the transport terms with integration by parts. Notice that these transport terms would not cause any trouble if we work in classical Sobolev spaces. In other words, the anti-symmetric terms in (
3.9
-
3.12
) are the only reason why we cannot prove Theorem
2.2
in classical Sobolev spaces.

3.
A priori energy estimate

In this section, we will derive the a priori energy estimate for equations (
2.15
-
2.16
).
Indeed, we look for the estimate of the type

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(\|\partial_{x}^{k}h\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\gamma(t)}}+\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\|\partial_{x}^{k}\theta\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\gamma(t)}}\right)+c_{0}\text{Diss}_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle-2\gamma^{\prime}(t)\tanh(2\gamma(t))\left(\|\Lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{x}^{k}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma(t))}}+\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\|\Lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{x}^{k}\theta\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma(t)}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\text{nonlinear terms}.

The term
\text{Diss}_{k}
stands for the dissipation term which we have mentioned in Remark
2.3
.
In order to get this inequality we will need to take
k
-derivatives in the equations for
h
and
\theta
and test with
\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}h}
and
\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}\theta}
. After integrating over
\Gamma_{\pm}(t)
, we will have to control terms of the form

\displaystyle P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm(t)}}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm(t)}}P(x,x_{1})\partial_{x}^{j}w(x)\partial_{x}^{k}w(x_{1})dxdx_{1}.

Here
w
stands for
h
or
\theta
, and
P
stands for an integral kernel satisfying
|P|\simeq\frac{1}{|x-x_{1}|}
which will be specified later for each term.
Depending on the numbers of the derivatives, i.e. depending on
j\in[0,k+1]\cap\mathbb{Z}
and the behaviour of the kernel
P
, we will split them in several classes:

(1)

Most singular terms and the transport term. They are the terms with
j=k+1
. Section
3.1
-
3.5
are devoted to them. In fact, in section
3.2
, we will show that the symmetric part of the most singular terms will give rise to the dissipation
\text{Diss}_{k}
.

(2)

Safe term. They are the terms with
j=k
. These terms may be controlled by using
\text{Diss}_{k}
, or controlled by
\|\partial_{x}^{k}w\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma(t)}}^{2}
through the T1 theorem, which will be presented in section
3.6
.

(3)

Easy term. They are the terms with
j<k
. These terms can be controlled by
\|h\|^{2}_{H^{k}_{\gamma(t)}}+\|\theta\|_{H^{k}_{\gamma(t)}}^{2}
, which is given in section
3.7
.

To begin with, applying
\partial_{x}^{k}
on each side of (
2.15
-
2.16
) yields that

(3.1)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\partial_{x}^{k}h+\left(\mu_{2}u_{+}+\mu_{1}u_{-}\right)\partial^{k+1}_{x}h+\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\left(u_{+}-u_{-}\right)\partial_{x}^{k+1}\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\mu_{2}^{2}P_{11}+\mu_{1}\mu_{2}P_{12}+\mu_{1}\mu_{2}P_{21}+\mu_{1}^{2}P_{22}\right)(x,x-\alpha)\partial^{k+1}_{x}h(x-\alpha)d\alpha

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\mu_{2}(P_{11}-P_{12})-\mu_{1}(P_{22}-P_{21})\right)(x,x-\alpha)\partial_{x}^{k+1}\theta(x-\alpha)d\alpha

\displaystyle+\sum_{m=1}^{4}N^{h}_{m}

(3.2)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\partial_{x}^{k}\theta+\left(u_{+}-u_{-}\right)\partial^{k+1}_{x}h+\left(\mu_{1}u_{+}+\mu_{2}u_{-}\right)\partial^{k+1}_{x}\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\mu_{2}(P_{11}-P_{21})-\mu_{1}(P_{22}-P_{12})\right)(x,x-\alpha)\partial^{k+1}_{x}h(x-\alpha)d\alpha

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(P_{11}+P_{22}-P_{12}-P_{21}\right)(x,x-\alpha)\partial_{x}^{k+1}\theta(x-\alpha)d\alpha

\displaystyle+\sum_{m=1}^{4}N^{\theta}_{m},

where
P_{ij}
are given by (
2.14
), and
N^{h}_{m}
and
N^{\theta}_{m}
are commutators given by

N_{1}^{h}:=-\left[\partial_{x}^{k},\mu_{2}u_{+}+\mu_{1}u_{-}\right]\partial_{x}h,\quad N_{2}^{h}:=-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\left[\partial_{x}^{k},u_{+}-u_{-}\right]\partial_{x}\theta,

N_{3}^{h}:=-\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\partial_{x}^{k},\left(\mu_{2}^{2}P_{11}+\mu_{1}\mu_{2}P_{12}+\mu_{1}\mu_{2}P_{21}+\mu_{1}^{2}P_{22}\right)(x,x-\alpha)\right]\partial_{x}h(x-\alpha)d\alpha,

N_{4}^{h}:=-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\partial_{x}^{k},\left(\mu_{2}(P_{11}-P_{12})-\mu_{1}(P_{22}-P_{21})\right)(x,x-\alpha)\right]\partial_{x}\theta(x-\alpha)d\alpha,

N_{1}^{\theta}:=-\left[\partial_{x}^{k},u_{+}-u_{-}\right]\partial_{x}h,\quad N_{2}^{\theta}:=-\left[\partial_{x}^{k},\mu_{1}u_{+}+\mu_{2}u_{-}\right]\partial_{x}\theta,

N_{3}^{\theta}:=-\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\partial_{x}^{k},\left(\mu_{2}(P_{11}-P_{21})-\mu_{1}(P_{22}-P_{12})\right)(x,x-\alpha)\right]\partial_{x}h(x-\alpha)d\alpha,

N_{4}^{\theta}:=-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\Delta\rho\,P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\partial_{x}^{k},\left(P_{11}+P_{22}-P_{12}-P_{21}\right)(x,x-\alpha)\right]\partial_{x}\theta(x-\alpha)d\alpha.

Then we multiply equation (
3.1
) by
\overline{\partial^{k}_{x}h}
, multiply equation (
3.2
) by
\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\overline{\partial^{k}_{x}\theta}
, and integrate the real part of the sum over
\Gamma_{\pm}(t):=\left\{x\in\mathbb{C}\mid x=x^{\prime}\pm i\gamma(t),\,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}\right\}.

To obtain the estimate over the resulted equation, we will look at the contribution of each term in (
3.1
)(
3.2
) in this section.

The organization of this section is as follows. In section
3.1
, the most singular terms—the contribution of the first two lines on the right-hand side of (
3.1
)(
3.2
) are decomposed into three classes after an integration by parts: the symmetric part, the anti-symmetric part and the lower order terms. In Section
3.2
, we prove that the symmetric part is negative definite and provides dissipation terms. The lower order terms are handled in Section
3.3
in an easier fashion. In Section
3.4
, using the properties of the Hilbert transform, we are able to control the anti-symmetric part with loss of half a derivative in both
h
and
\theta
. The second and third terms on the left-hand side of (
3.1
)(
3.2
), which we refer to as transport terms, are treated in section
3.5
. Then we control the commutators
N_{m}^{h}
and
N_{m}^{\theta}
in Section
3.6
and
3.7
. The bounds obtained above are collected to complete the energy estimate in Section
3.8
and
3.9
.

3.1.
Decomposition of the most singular terms

In this section, the most singular terms are decomposed into the aforementioned three classes. Recall that

\Gamma_{\pm}(t)=\left\{x\in\mathbb{C}\mid x=x^{\prime}\pm i\gamma(t),\,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}\right\}.

For
x\in\Gamma_{\pm}(t)
and
(i,j)\in\left\{1,2\right\}^{2}
, applying the change of variable
x_{1}=x-\alpha
and integrating by parts gives

(3.3)

\displaystyle P.V.\int_{\mathbb{R}}P_{ij}(x,x-\alpha)\partial_{x}^{k+1}h(x-\alpha)d\alpha

\displaystyle=P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}P_{ij}(x,x_{1})\partial_{x}^{k+1}h(x_{1})dx_{1}

\displaystyle=P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\partial_{x_{1}}P_{ij}(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h(x,x_{1})\,dx_{1}.

Then we compute that

\partial_{x_{1}}P_{11}(x,x_{1})=K_{11}(x,x_{1})+J_{11}(x,x_{1}),

K_{11}(x,x_{1}):=\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta f)^{2}},\quad J_{11}(x,x_{1}):=\frac{2\Delta f(\partial_{x}f(x_{1})\Delta x-\Delta f)}{\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta f)^{2}\right)^{2}}.

\partial_{x_{1}}P_{22}(x,x_{1})=K_{22}(x,x_{1})+J_{22}(x,x_{1}),

K_{22}(x,x_{1}):=\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta g)^{2}},\quad J_{22}(x,x_{1}):=\frac{2\Delta g(\partial_{x}g(x_{1})\Delta x-\Delta g)}{\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta g)^{2}\right)}.

For
\partial_{x_{1}}P_{12}
and
\partial_{x_{1}}P_{21}
, we will use two equivalent expressions for each of them:

\partial_{x_{1}}P_{12}(x,x_{1})=K_{12}(x,x_{1})+J_{12}(x,x_{1})=\tilde{K}_{12}(x,x_{1})+\tilde{J}_{12}(x,x_{1}),

\partial_{x_{1}}P_{21}(x,x_{1})=K_{21}(x,x_{1})+J_{21}(x,x_{1})=\tilde{K}_{21}(x,x_{1})+\tilde{J}_{21}(x,x_{1}),

K_{12}(x,x_{1}):=\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta f)^{2}-(2\sigma+\theta(x_{1}))^{2}}{\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta f+2\sigma+\theta(x_{1}))^{2}\right)^{2}},\quad\tilde{K}_{12}(x,x_{1}):=\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta g)^{2}-(2\sigma+\theta(x))^{2}}{\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta g+2\sigma+\theta(x))^{2}\right)^{2}},

J_{12}(x,x_{1}):=\frac{2(\Delta f+2\sigma+\theta(x_{1}))(\partial_{x}f(x_{1})\Delta x-\Delta f)}{\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta f+2\sigma+\theta(x_{1}))^{2}\right)^{2}}-\frac{2\Delta x\partial_{x}\theta(x_{1})(\Delta f+2\sigma+\theta(x_{1}))}{\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta f+2\sigma+\theta(x_{1}))^{2}\right)^{2}},

\tilde{J}_{12}(x,x_{1})=\frac{2(\Delta g+2\sigma+\theta(x))(\partial_{x}g(x_{1})\Delta x-\Delta g)}{\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta g+2\sigma+\theta(x))^{2}\right)^{2}}.

K_{21}(x,x_{1}):=\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta f)^{2}-(2\sigma+\theta(x))^{2}}{\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta f-2\sigma-\theta(x))^{2}\right)^{2}},\quad\tilde{K}_{21}(x,x_{1}):=\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta g)^{2}-(2\sigma+\theta(x_{1}))^{2}}{\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta g-2\sigma-\theta(x_{1}))^{2}\right)^{2}},

J_{21}(x,x_{1}):=\frac{2(\Delta f-2\sigma-\theta(x))(\partial_{x}f(x_{1})\Delta x-\Delta f)}{\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta f-2\sigma-\theta(x))^{2}\right)^{2}},

\tilde{J}_{21}(x,x_{1}):=\frac{2(\Delta g-2\sigma-\theta(x_{1}))(\partial_{x}g(x_{1})\Delta x-\Delta g)}{\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta g-2\sigma-\theta(x_{1}))^{2}\right)^{2}}+\frac{2\Delta x\partial_{x}\theta(x_{1})(\Delta g-2\sigma-\theta(x_{1}))}{\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta g-2\sigma-\theta(x_{1}))^{2}\right)^{2}}.

Actually we will proof the following lemma in this section.

Lemma 3.1
.

The contribution of the most singular terms can be decomposed into the symmetric parts, the anti-symmetric parts and the lower order terms:

\displaystyle-\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(\mu_{2}^{2}P_{11}+\mu_{1}\mu_{2}(P_{12}+P_{21})+\mu_{1}^{2}P_{22}\right)(x,x_{1})\partial_{x}^{k+1}h(x_{1})dx_{1}\,\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}h(x)}dx

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}^{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}(P_{11}-P_{12})(x,x_{1})\partial_{x}^{k+1}\theta(x_{1})dx_{1}\,\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}h(x)}dx

\displaystyle+\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}(P_{22}-P_{21})(x,x_{1})\partial_{x}^{k+1}\theta(x_{1})dx_{1}\,\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}h(x)}dx

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}^{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(P_{11}+P_{22}-P_{12}-P_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\partial_{x}^{k+1}\theta(x_{1})dx_{1}\,\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}\theta(x)}dx

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}^{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}(P_{11}-P_{21})(x,x_{1})\partial_{x}^{k+1}h(x_{1})dx_{1}\,\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}\theta(x)}dx

\displaystyle+\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}(P_{22}-P_{12})(x,x_{1})\partial_{x}^{k+1}h(x_{1})dx_{1}\,\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}\theta(x)}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{sym}+T_{low}+T_{asym},

where
T_{sym}
,
T_{asym}
and
T_{low}
denote the symmetric part, anti-symmetric part and the lower order terms respectively. They are given as follows.

\displaystyle T_{sym}:=

\displaystyle-\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left[\frac{1}{2}\Re D_{11}|\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h|^{2}\right.

\displaystyle+\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\Re\left(\mu_{2}\left(K_{11}-\frac{1}{2}K_{12}-\frac{1}{2}K_{21}\right)-\mu_{1}\left(K_{22}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{12}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{21}\right)\right)\Re\left(\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\cdot\overline{\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta}\right)

\displaystyle+\left.\frac{\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}^{2}}{2}\Re\left(K_{11}-\frac{1}{2}K_{12}-\frac{1}{2}K_{21}+K_{22}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{12}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{21}\right)|\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta|^{2}\right]dxdx_{1},

where we denote

D_{11}(x,x_{1}):=\left(\mu_{2}^{2}K_{11}+\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}\mu_{2}K_{12}+\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}\mu_{2}K_{21}+\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\tilde{K}_{12}+\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\tilde{K}_{21}+\mu_{1}^{2}K_{22}\right)(x,x_{1}).

\displaystyle T_{low}:=

\displaystyle-\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(\mu_{2}^{2}J_{11}+\mu_{1}^{2}J_{22}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\cdot\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}h(x)}dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle-\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\frac{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}{2}\left(J_{12}+J_{21}+\tilde{J}_{12}+\tilde{J}_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\cdot\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}h(x)}dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}^{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left[J_{11}+J_{22}-\frac{1}{2}\left(J_{12}+J_{21}+\tilde{J}_{12}+\tilde{J}_{21}\right)\right](x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta\cdot\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}\theta(x)}dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle+\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(J_{22}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{J}_{12}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{J}_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta\cdot\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}h(x)}dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}^{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(J_{11}-\frac{1}{2}J_{12}-\frac{1}{2}J_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta\cdot\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}h(x)}dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}^{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(J_{11}-\frac{1}{2}J_{12}-\frac{1}{2}J_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\cdot\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}\theta(x)}dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle+\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(J_{22}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{J}_{12}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{J}_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\cdot\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}\theta(x)}dxdx_{1}.

\displaystyle T_{asym}:=

\displaystyle\mu_{1}\mu_{2}^{2}P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\frac{1}{2}(P_{12}-P_{21})(x,x_{1})\partial_{x}^{k+1}\theta(x_{1})\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}h(x)}dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle+\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\frac{1}{2}(P_{12}-P_{21})(x,x_{1})\partial_{x}^{k+1}\theta(x_{1})\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}h(x)}dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}^{2}P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\frac{1}{2}(P_{12}-P_{21})(x,x_{1})\partial_{x}^{k+1}h(x_{1})\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}\theta(x)}dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\frac{1}{2}(P_{12}-P_{21})(x,x_{1})\partial_{x}^{k+1}h(x_{1})\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}\theta(x)}dxdx_{1}.

Proof of Lemma
3.1
.

To derive the above decomposition, by (
3.3
) the terms from (
3.1
) involving the highest order derivative of
h
are

(3.4)

\displaystyle-\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(\mu_{2}^{2}P_{11}+\mu_{1}\mu_{2}(P_{12}+P_{21})+\mu_{1}^{2}P_{22}\right)(x,x_{1})\partial_{x}^{k+1}h(x_{1})dx_{1}\,\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}h(x)}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}D_{11}(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\cdot\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}h(x)}dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle-\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(\mu_{2}^{2}J_{11}+\mu_{1}^{2}J_{22}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\cdot\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}h(x)}dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle-\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\frac{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}{2}\left(J_{12}+J_{21}+\tilde{J}_{12}+\tilde{J}_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\cdot\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}h(x)}dxdx_{1}.

By noticing
K_{12}(x,x_{1})=K_{21}(x_{1},x)
and
\tilde{K}_{12}(x,x_{1})=\tilde{K}_{21}(x,x_{1})
, we see that
K_{11}
,
K_{22}
,
K_{12}+K_{21}
and
\tilde{K}_{12}+\tilde{K}_{21}
are symmetric in the sense that

K_{11}(x,x_{1})=K_{11}(x_{1},x),\quad K_{22}(x,x_{1})=K_{22}(x_{1},x),

(K_{12}+K_{21})(x,x_{1})=(K_{12}+K_{21})(x_{1},x),\quad(\tilde{K}_{12}+\tilde{K}_{21})(x,x_{1})=(\tilde{K}_{12}+\tilde{K}_{21})(x,x_{1}).

In particular,
D_{11}(x,x_{1})=D_{11}(x_{1},x)
. Hence

(3.5)

\displaystyle\Re\,P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}D_{11}(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\cdot\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}h(x)}dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Re\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}D_{11}(x,x_{1})\left|\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\right|^{2}dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\Re D_{11}(x,x_{1})\left|\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\right|^{2}dxdx_{1}.

Replacing
h
by
\theta
in (
3.3
), the terms from (
3.2
) involving the highest order of
\theta
are

(3.6)

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}^{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(P_{11}+P_{22}-P_{12}-P_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\partial_{x}^{k+1}\theta(x_{1})dx_{1}\,\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}\theta(x)}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}^{2}P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(K_{11}-\frac{1}{2}K_{12}-\frac{1}{2}K_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta\cdot\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}\theta(x)}dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}^{2}P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(K_{22}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{12}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta\cdot\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}\theta(x)}dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}^{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(J_{11}+J_{22}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta\cdot\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}\theta(x)}dxdx_{1}.

\displaystyle+\frac{\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}^{2}}{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}(J_{12}+J_{21}+\tilde{J}_{12}+\tilde{J}_{21})(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta\cdot\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}\theta(x)}dxdx_{1}.

By symmetry, it follows

(3.7)

\displaystyle\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}^{2}\Re\,P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(K_{11}-\frac{1}{2}K_{12}-\frac{1}{2}K_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta\cdot\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}\theta(x)}dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}^{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\Re\left(K_{11}-\frac{1}{2}K_{12}-\frac{1}{2}K_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})|\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta|^{2}dxdx_{1},

(3.8)

\displaystyle\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}^{2}\Re\,P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(K_{22}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{12}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta\cdot\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}\theta(x)}dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}^{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\Re\left(K_{22}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{12}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})|\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta|^{2}dxdx_{1}.

For the terms from (
3.1
) involving the highest order derivative of
\theta
, we decompose them into symmetric parts and anti-symmetric parts:

P_{11}-P_{12}=\left(P_{11}-\frac{1}{2}P_{12}-\frac{1}{2}P_{21}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\left(P_{12}-P_{21}\right),

P_{22}-P_{21}=\left(P_{22}-\frac{1}{2}P_{12}-\frac{1}{2}P_{21}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(P_{12}-P_{21}\right).

Then use (
3.3
) to get

(3.9)

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}^{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}(P_{11}-P_{12})(x,x_{1})\partial_{x}^{k+1}\theta(x_{1})dx_{1}\,\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}h(x)}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}\mu_{2}^{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(K_{11}-\frac{1}{2}K_{12}-\frac{1}{2}K_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta\cdot\overline{\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h}\,dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}^{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(J_{11}-\frac{1}{2}J_{12}-\frac{1}{2}J_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta\cdot\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}h(x)}dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle+\mu_{1}\mu_{2}^{2}P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\frac{1}{2}(P_{12}-P_{21})(x,x_{1})\partial_{x}^{k+1}\theta(x_{1})\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}h(x)}dxdx_{1}

(3.10)

\displaystyle\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}(P_{22}-P_{21})(x,x_{1})\partial_{x}^{k+1}\theta(x_{1})dx_{1}\,\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}h(x)}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(K_{22}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{12}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta\cdot\overline{\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h}\,dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle+\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(J_{22}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{J}_{12}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{J}_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta\cdot\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}h(x)}dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle+\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\frac{1}{2}(P_{12}-P_{21})(x,x_{1})\partial_{x}^{k+1}\theta(x_{1})\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}h(x)}dxdx_{1}.

Similarly, for the terms from (
3.2
) involving the highest order derivative of
h
, we apply the decomposition

P_{11}-P_{21}=\left(P_{11}-\frac{1}{2}P_{12}-\frac{1}{2}P_{21}\right)+\frac{1}{2}(P_{12}-P_{21})
and

P_{22}-P_{12}=\left(P_{22}-\frac{1}{2}P_{12}-\frac{1}{2}P_{21}\right)-\frac{1}{2}(P_{12}-P_{21})

so that

(3.11)

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}^{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}(P_{11}-P_{21})(x,x_{1})\partial_{x}^{k+1}h(x_{1})dx_{1}\,\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}\theta(x)}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}\mu_{2}^{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(K_{11}-\frac{1}{2}K_{12}-\frac{1}{2}K_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\cdot\overline{\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta}\,dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}^{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(J_{11}-\frac{1}{2}J_{12}-\frac{1}{2}J_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\cdot\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}\theta(x)}dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}^{2}P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\frac{1}{2}(P_{12}-P_{21})(x,x_{1})\partial_{x}^{k+1}h(x_{1})\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}\theta(x)}dxdx_{1},

(3.12)

\displaystyle\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}P.V.\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}(P_{22}-P_{12})(x,x_{1})\partial_{x}^{k+1}h(x_{1})dx_{1}\,\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}\theta(x)}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(K_{22}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{12}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\cdot\overline{\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta}\,dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle+\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(J_{22}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{J}_{12}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{J}_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\cdot\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}\theta(x)}dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\frac{1}{2}(P_{12}-P_{21})(x,x_{1})\partial_{x}^{k+1}h(x_{1})\overline{\partial_{x}^{k}\theta(x)}dxdx_{1}.

Moreover, the real part of the sum of the
K
’s terms in (
3.9
) and (
3.11
) is

(3.13)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}\mu_{2}^{2}\Re\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(K_{11}-\frac{1}{2}K_{12}-\frac{1}{2}K_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta\cdot\overline{\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h}\,dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}\mu_{2}^{2}\Re\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(K_{11}-\frac{1}{2}K_{12}-\frac{1}{2}K_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\cdot\overline{\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta}\,dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mu_{1}\mu_{2}^{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\Re\left(K_{11}-\frac{1}{2}K_{12}-\frac{1}{2}K_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Re\left(\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\cdot\overline{\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta}\right)dxdx_{1},

while the real part of the sum of the
K
’s terms in (
3.10
) and (
3.12
) is

(3.14)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}\Re\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(K_{22}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{12}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta\cdot\overline{\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h}\,dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}\Re\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\left(K_{22}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{12}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\cdot\overline{\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta}\,dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\Re\left(K_{22}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{12}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})\cdot\Re\left(\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\cdot\overline{\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta}\right)dxdx_{1}.

Summing up equations (
3.4
)(
3.6
) and (
3.9
-
3.12
), we arrive at the desired decomposition in Lemma
3.1
.
∎

3.2.
Symmetric part and parabolicity

The symmetric part
T_{sym}
consists of an integral of a quadratic form of
\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h
and
\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta
:

(3.15)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Re D_{11}|\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h|^{2}+\frac{\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}^{2}}{2}\Re\left(K_{11}-\frac{1}{2}K_{12}-\frac{1}{2}K_{21}+K_{22}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{12}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{21}\right)|\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta|^{2}

\displaystyle+\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\Re\left(\mu_{2}\left(K_{11}-\frac{1}{2}K_{12}-\frac{1}{2}K_{21}\right)-\mu_{1}\left(K_{22}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{12}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{21}\right)\right)\Re\left(\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\cdot\overline{\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta}\right).

The goal of this section is to prove that this quadratic form is positive definite up to some other lower order terms, and therefore the symmetric part
T_{sym}
gives rise to a dissipation term. The main result of this section is as follows.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
D_{11}^{0}
and
D_{22}^{0}
be given by

D_{11}^{0}(x,x_{1})=\mu_{2}^{2}\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}}+2\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}-(2\sigma)^{2}}{\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}\right)^{2}}+\mu_{1}^{2}\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}},

D_{22}^{0}(x,x_{1})=2G_{0}(\Delta x,2\sigma)^{-1}F_{0}(\Delta x,2\sigma),

where

G_{0}(\Delta x,2\sigma)=(\Delta x)^{2}\left[(\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}\right]^{4},

F_{0}(\Delta x,2\sigma)=(2\sigma)^{8}+5(\Delta x)^{2}(2\sigma)^{6}+7(\Delta x)^{4}(2\sigma)^{4}+3(\Delta x)^{6}(2\sigma)^{2}.

The symmetric part
T_{sym}
satisfies the following control.

\displaystyle-T_{sym}\geq

\displaystyle C\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}D_{11}^{0}(x,x_{1})|\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h|^{2}dxdx_{1}+C\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}^{2}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}(t)}D_{22}^{0}(x,x_{1})|\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta|^{2}dxdx_{1}

\displaystyle-C\sigma^{-1}\|\partial_{x}\theta\|_{L^{\infty}_{\gamma(t)}}\left(\|\partial_{x}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{\gamma(t)}}+\|\partial_{x}g\|_{L^{\infty}_{\gamma(t)}}\right)\|\partial_{x}^{k}h\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma(t)}}\|\partial_{x}^{k}\theta\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma(t)}}

\displaystyle-C\sigma^{-1}\|\partial_{x}\theta\|_{L^{\infty}_{\gamma(t)}}\left(\|\partial_{x}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{\gamma(t)}}+\|\partial_{x}g\|_{L^{\infty}_{\gamma(t)}}\right)\|\partial_{x}^{k}\theta\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma(t)}}^{2}.

Remark 3.3
.

As a complement of Remark
2.3
, the lower bound of
-T_{sym}
given by Lemma
3.2
shows that the parabolicity of the equations (
2.15
)(
2.16
) gives rise to not only dissipation in
h
but also in
\theta
. These dissipation terms will be used to cancel other terms from section
3.3
, section
3.6
and section
3.7
, see Lemma
3.8
and Lemma
3.14
-
3.17
. Moreover, the lower bound of
-T_{sym}
allows us to choose
-\gamma^{\prime}
as small as it is given in (
2.22
) instead of constant size, see also Remark
2.4
.

In order to prove Lemma
3.2
, the first step is decomposing
K_{11}-\frac{1}{2}K_{12}-\frac{1}{2}K_{21}
and
K_{22}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{12}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{21}
into their principal parts and lower order parts.

Lemma 3.4
.

There exist functions
K_{f}
,
K_{g}
,
e_{f}
,
e_{g}
such that

(1)
K_{11}-\frac{1}{2}K_{12}-\frac{1}{2}K_{21}=K_{f}+e_{f}
,
K_{22}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{12}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{21}=K_{g}+e_{g}
.

(2)
K_{f}
and
K_{g}
are perturbations of positive rational functions of
\Delta x
and
2\sigma
:

K_{f}(x,x_{1})=G\left(\Delta x,2\sigma;\frac{\Delta f}{\Delta x},\frac{\theta(x)}{2\sigma},\frac{\theta(x_{1})}{2\sigma}\right)^{-1}F\left(\Delta x,2\sigma;\frac{\Delta f}{\Delta x},\frac{\theta(x)}{2\sigma},\frac{\theta(x_{1})}{2\sigma}\right),

K_{g}(x,x_{1})=G\left(\Delta x,2\sigma;\frac{\Delta g}{\Delta x},\frac{\theta(x_{1})}{2\sigma},\frac{\theta(x)}{2\sigma}\right)^{-1}F\left(\Delta x,2\sigma;\frac{\Delta g}{\Delta x},\frac{\theta(x_{1})}{2\sigma},\frac{\theta(x)}{2\sigma}\right).

(3)
e_{f}
and
e_{g}
have
\Delta\theta
as a factor:

e_{f}(x,x_{1})=\Delta\theta\Delta f\cdot G\left(\Delta x,2\sigma;\frac{\Delta f}{\Delta x},\frac{\theta(x)}{2\sigma},\frac{\theta(x_{1})}{2\sigma}\right)^{-1}E\left(\Delta x,2\sigma;\frac{\Delta f}{\Delta x},\frac{\theta(x)}{2\sigma},\frac{\theta(x_{1})}{2\sigma}\right),

e_{g}(x,x_{1})=-\Delta\theta\Delta g\cdot G\left(\Delta x,2\sigma;\frac{\Delta g}{\Delta x},\frac{\theta(x_{1})}{2\sigma},\frac{\theta(x)}{2\sigma}\right)^{-1}E\left(\Delta x,2\sigma;\frac{\Delta g}{\Delta x},\frac{\theta(x_{1})}{2\sigma},\frac{\theta(x)}{2\sigma}\right).

Here
E\left(\Delta x,2\sigma;w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}\right)
,
F\left(\Delta x,2\sigma;w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}\right)
,
G\left(\Delta x,2\sigma;w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}\right)
are two-element polynomials of
\Delta x
and
2\sigma
with coefficients depending on
(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})
. Moreover,
G(\Delta x,2\sigma;0,0,0)\geq 0
,
F(\Delta x,2\sigma;0,0,0)\geq 0
.

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle G\left(\Delta x,2\sigma;,w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}\right)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left(1+w_{1}^{2}\right)\left(\Delta x\right)^{2}\left[\left(\Delta x\right)^{2}+\left(w_{1}\Delta x+2(1+w_{3})\sigma\right)^{2}\right]^{2}\left[\left(\Delta x\right)^{2}+\left(-w_{1}\Delta x+2(1+w_{2})\sigma\right)^{2}\right]^{2},

so that

\displaystyle G\left(\Delta x,2\sigma;\frac{\Delta f}{\Delta x},\frac{\theta(x)}{2\sigma},\frac{\theta(x_{1})}{2\sigma}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta f)^{2}\right)\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta f+2\sigma+\theta(x_{1}))^{2}\right)^{2}\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(-\Delta f+2\sigma+\theta(x))^{2}\right)^{2}.

Then we compute by brute force to get

\displaystyle G\left(\Delta x,2\sigma;\frac{\Delta f}{\Delta x},\frac{\theta(x)}{2\sigma},\frac{\theta(x_{1})}{2\sigma}\right)\left(K_{11}-\frac{1}{2}K_{12}-\frac{1}{2}K_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{0}(2\sigma)^{8}+c_{2}(\Delta x)^{2}(2\sigma)^{6}+c_{4}(\Delta x)^{4}(2\sigma)^{4}+c_{6}(\Delta x)^{6}(2\sigma)^{2}

\displaystyle+c_{1}(\Delta x)(2\sigma)^{7}+c_{3}(\Delta x)^{3}(2\sigma)^{5}+c_{5}(\Delta x)^{5}(2\sigma)^{3}+c_{7}(\Delta x)^{7}(2\sigma),

where
c_{j}=c_{j}\left(\frac{\Delta f}{\Delta x},\frac{\theta(x)}{2\sigma},\frac{\theta(x_{1})}{2\sigma}\right)
,
0\leq j\leq 8
are functions of
\left(\frac{\Delta f}{\Delta x},\frac{\theta(x)}{2\sigma},\frac{\theta(x_{1})}{2\sigma}\right)
:

c_{0}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})=(1+w_{2})^{4}(1+w_{3})^{4},

\displaystyle c_{2}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(5-3w_{1}^{2})(1+w_{2})^{2}(1+w_{3})^{2}\left[(1+w_{2})^{2}+(1+w_{3})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle+8w_{1}^{2}(1+w_{2})^{2}(1+w_{3})^{2}(w_{2}-w_{3})^{2},

\displaystyle c_{4}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(1+w_{1})^{2}(1-15w_{1})^{2}\left[(1+w_{2})^{4}+(1+w_{3})^{4}\right]

\displaystyle+(6+16w_{1}^{2}+34w_{1}^{4})(1+w_{2}^{2})(1+w_{3}^{2})

\displaystyle+8w_{1}^{2}(1+w_{1}^{2})\left[(1+w_{2})^{2}+(1+w_{3})^{2}\right](w_{2}-w_{3})^{2},

\displaystyle c_{6}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})=\frac{3}{2}(1+w_{1}^{2})^{2}(1-3w_{1}^{2})\left[(1+w_{2})^{2}+(1+w_{3})^{2}\right]+8w_{1}^{2}(1+w_{1}^{2})^{2}(w_{2}-w_{3})^{2},

c_{1}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})=4w_{1}(w_{2}-w_{3})(1+w_{2})^{3}(1+w_{3})^{3},

\displaystyle c_{3}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})=

\displaystyle w_{1}(w_{2}-w_{3})\left[-(10+26w_{1}^{2})(1+w_{2})^{2}(1+w_{3})^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+4(1+w_{1}^{2})(1+w_{2})(1+w_{3})\frac{(1+w_{2})^{3}-(1+w_{3})^{3}}{w_{2}-w_{3}}\right],

\displaystyle c_{5}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})

\displaystyle=w_{1}(w_{2}-w_{3})\left[2(1+w_{1}^{2})^{2}\frac{(1+w_{2})^{3}-(1+w_{3})^{3}}{w_{2}-w_{3}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+(1+w_{1}^{2})(10+26w_{1}^{2})(1+w_{2})(1+w_{3})\right],

\displaystyle c_{7}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})=-2w_{1}(w_{2}-w_{3})(1+w_{1}^{2})^{3}.

Now we let

F(\Delta x,2\sigma;w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}):=\sum_{j=0}^{3}c_{2j}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})(\Delta x)^{2j}(2\sigma)^{8-2j},

E(\Delta x,2\sigma;w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}):=w_{1}^{-1}(w_{2}-w_{3})^{-1}\sum_{j=0}^{3}c_{1+2j}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})(\Delta x)^{2j}(2\sigma)^{6-2j}.

Note that
c_{1+2j}
,
j=0,1,2,3
have
w_{1}(w_{2}-w_{3})
as a factor, so
E
is well-defined. Then we get

\left(K_{11}-\frac{1}{2}K_{12}-\frac{1}{2}K_{21}\right)(x,x_{1})=K_{f}(x,x_{1})+e_{f}(x,x_{1}),

\displaystyle K_{f}(x,x_{1})=

\displaystyle G\left(\Delta x,2\sigma;\frac{\Delta f}{\Delta x},\frac{\theta(x)}{2\sigma},\frac{\theta(x_{1})}{2\sigma}\right)^{-1}F\left(\Delta x,2\sigma;\frac{\Delta f}{\Delta x},\frac{\theta(x)}{2\sigma},\frac{\theta(x_{1})}{2\sigma}\right),

\displaystyle e_{f}(x,x_{1})=\Delta\theta\Delta f\cdot G\left(\Delta x,2\sigma;\frac{\Delta f}{\Delta x},\frac{\theta(x)}{2\sigma},\frac{\theta(x_{1})}{2\sigma}\right)^{-1}E\left(\Delta x,2\sigma;\frac{\Delta f}{\Delta x},\frac{\theta(x)}{2\sigma},\frac{\theta(x_{1})}{2\sigma}\right).

To obtain the corresponding identities for
K_{22}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{12}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{21}
, it suffices to observe that the expression of
K_{22}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{12}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{21}
is the same as
K_{11}-\frac{1}{2}K_{12}-\frac{1}{2}K_{21}
if we replace
\Delta g
by
\Delta f
and swap
\theta(x)
and
\theta(x_{1})
. Finally, we see that

G_{0}(\Delta x,2\sigma):=G(\Delta x,2\sigma;0,0,0)=(\Delta x)^{2}\left[(\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}\right]^{4}\geq 0,

F_{0}(\Delta x,2\sigma):=F(\Delta x,2\sigma;0,0,0)=(2\sigma)^{8}+5(\Delta x)^{2}(2\sigma)^{6}+7(\Delta x)^{4}(2\sigma)^{4}+3(\Delta x)^{6}(2\sigma)^{2}\geq 0.

∎

Using Lemma
3.4
, we decompose the quadratic form (
3.15
) into its principal part and lower-order parts:

(3.16)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Re D_{11}|\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h|^{2}+\frac{\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}^{2}}{2}\Re\left(K_{11}-\frac{1}{2}K_{12}-\frac{1}{2}K_{21}+K_{22}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{12}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{21}\right)|\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta|^{2}

\displaystyle+\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\Re\left(\mu_{2}\left(K_{11}-\frac{1}{2}K_{12}-\frac{1}{2}K_{21}\right)-\mu_{1}\left(K_{22}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{12}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{K}_{21}\right)\right)\Re\left(\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\cdot\overline{\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Re D_{11}|\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h|^{2}+\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\Re D_{12}\cdot\Re\left(\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\cdot\overline{\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta}\right)+\frac{\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}^{2}}{2}\Re D_{22}|\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta|^{2}

\displaystyle+\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\Re\left(\mu_{2}e_{f}-\mu_{1}e_{g}\right)\cdot\Re\left(\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\cdot\overline{\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta}\right)+\frac{\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}^{2}}{2}\Re\left(e_{f}+e_{g}\right)|\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta|^{2},

where we denote

D_{12}:=\mu_{2}K_{f}-\mu_{1}K_{g}
,
D_{22}=K_{f}+K_{g}.

The last two terms on the right-hand side of (
3.16
) are considered lower order for that
e_{f}
and
e_{g}
have
\Delta\theta
as a factor, which is expected to be proportional to
\sigma\Delta x
. In the next, we show that the principle part is positive definite for small data, for which we need the following algebraic inequality.

Lemma 3.5
.

Suppose that
a_{j}
and
b_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq m
are complex valued. Then we have the inequality

\left|\prod_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}-\prod_{j=1}^{n}b_{j}\right|\leq\prod_{j=1}^{n}\max\left\{|a_{j}|,|b_{j}|\right\}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{|a_{j}-b_{j}|}{\max\left\{|a_{j}|,|b_{j}|\right\}}.

Proof.

We prove the desired inequality by induction. The case
n=1
is trivial. Suppose that the above inequality holds for
n-1
. Then

\displaystyle\left|\prod_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}-\prod_{j=1}^{n}b_{j}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|a_{n}-b_{n}|\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}|a_{j}|+|b_{n}|\left|\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}a_{j}-\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}b_{j}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\max\left\{|a_{n}|,|b_{n}|\right\}\frac{|a_{n}-b_{n}|}{\max\left\{|a_{n}|,|b_{n}|\right\}}\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}|a_{j}|+|b_{n}|\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\frac{|a_{j}-b_{j}|}{\max\left\{|a_{j}|,|b_{j}|\right\}}\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\max\left\{|a_{j}|,|b_{j}|\right\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\max\left\{|a_{j}|,|b_{j}|\right\}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{|a_{j}-b_{j}|}{\max\left\{|a_{j}|,|b_{j}|\right\}}.

∎

Proposition 3.6
.

There exists a small constant
w_{0}>0
such that if

(3.17)

\|\partial_{x}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{\gamma}}+\|\partial_{x}g\|_{L^{\infty}_{\gamma}}+\sigma^{-1}\left\|\theta\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{\gamma}}\leq w_{0},

then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Re D_{11}|\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h|^{2}+\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\Re D_{12}\Re\left(\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h\cdot\overline{\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}^{2}\Re D_{22}|\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{7}{50}-Cw_{0}\right)D_{11}^{0}|\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}h|^{2}+\left(\frac{7}{64}-Cw_{0}\right)\mu_{1}^{2}\mu_{2}^{2}D_{22}^{0}|\Delta\partial_{x}^{k}\theta|^{2}.

Here
D_{11}^{0}
and
D_{22}^{0}
are given by (
3.21
)(
3.22
), and satisfy the following estimates:

D_{11}^{0}(x,x_{1})\simeq\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}},\quad D_{22}^{0}(x,x_{1})\simeq\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\frac{(2\sigma)^{2}}{(\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}}.

We remark that the two constants
\frac{7}{50}
and
\frac{7}{64}
are neither essential nor sharp. In the subsequent proof, we will only use the fact that the two leading coefficients in the lower bound given by Proposition
3.6
are positive.

To prove the desired inequality, we look at the determinant of the quadratic form:

(3.18)

\displaystyle\Re D_{11}\Re D_{22}-(\Re D_{12})^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Re\left(D_{11}-\mu_{2}^{2}K_{f}-\mu_{1}^{2}K_{g}\right)\cdot\Re(K_{f}+K_{g})+\Re K_{f}\cdot\Re K_{g}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Re K_{f}\cdot\Re\left(\frac{1}{2}K_{g}+D_{11}-\mu_{2}^{2}K_{f}-\mu_{1}^{2}K_{g}\right)+\Re K_{g}\cdot\Re\left(\frac{1}{2}K_{f}+D_{11}-\mu_{2}^{2}K_{f}-\mu_{1}^{2}K_{g}\right).

By Lemma
3.4
,

(3.19)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}K_{g}+D_{11}-\mu_{2}^{2}K_{f}-\mu_{1}^{2}K_{g}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}K_{22}+\frac{1}{2}\mu_{2}(K_{12}+K_{21})+\left(\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}-\frac{1}{4}\right)(\tilde{K}_{12}+\tilde{K}_{21})+\mu_{2}^{2}e_{f}+\left(\mu_{1}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\right)e_{g}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}K_{22}+\frac{1}{4}\tilde{K}_{12}+\frac{1}{4}\tilde{K}_{21}+\frac{1}{2}\mu_{2}(K_{12}+K_{21}-\tilde{K}_{12}-\tilde{K}_{21})+\mu_{2}^{2}e_{f}+\left(\mu_{1}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\right)e_{g},

and similarly,

(3.20)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}K_{f}+D_{11}-\mu_{2}^{2}K_{f}-\mu_{1}^{2}K_{g}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}K_{11}+\left(\frac{1}{2}\mu_{2}-\frac{1}{4}\right)(K_{12}+K_{21})+\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}(\tilde{K}_{12}+\tilde{K}_{21})+\left(\mu_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\right)e_{f}+\mu_{1}^{2}e_{g}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}K_{11}+\frac{1}{4}K_{12}+\frac{1}{4}K_{21}-\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}(K_{12}+K_{21}-\tilde{K}_{12}-\tilde{K}_{21})+\left(\mu_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\right)e_{f}+\mu_{1}^{2}e_{g}.

In order to prove Proposition
3.6
, the key point is to find a lower bound for the determinant. To this end, let
K_{f}^{0}
and
K_{g}^{0}
denote the unperturbed versions of
K_{f}
and
K_{g}
:

K_{f}^{0}(x,x_{1})=K_{g}^{0}(x,x_{1}):=G_{0}(\Delta x,2\sigma)^{-1}F_{0}(\Delta x,2\sigma),

where
F
and
G
are given by Lemma
3.4
.
Also, we denote
D_{11}^{0}
and
D_{22}^{0}
the unperturbed versions of
D_{11}
and
D_{22}
:

(3.21)

D_{11}^{0}(x,x_{1})=\mu_{2}^{2}\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}}+2\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}-(2\sigma)^{2}}{\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}\right)^{2}}+\mu_{1}^{2}\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}},

(3.22)

D_{22}^{0}(x,x_{1}):=K_{f}^{0}+K_{g}^{0}=2G_{0}(\Delta x,2\sigma)^{-1}F_{0}(\Delta x,2\sigma).

We will need the following estimates.

Lemma 3.7
.

Suppose that condition (
3.17
) holds. Then the following estimates hold.

(i)
\Re K_{f}
and
\Re K_{g}
are perturbations of
K_{f}^{0}
and
K_{g}^{0}
:

(1-Cw_{0})K_{f}^{0}(x,x_{1})\leq\Re K_{f}(x,x_{1})\leq(1+Cw_{0})K_{f}^{0}(x,x_{1}),

(1-Cw_{0})K_{g}^{0}(x,x_{1})\leq\Re K_{g}(x,x_{1})\leq(1+Cw_{0})K_{g}^{0}(x,x_{1}).

In particular,

(3.23)

\frac{1}{2}-Cw_{0}\leq\frac{\Re K_{f}}{\Re K_{f}+\Re K_{g}}\leq\frac{1}{2}+Cw_{0}.

(ii)
D_{11}^{0}
can be bounded from below and above:

(3.24)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\geq D_{11}^{0}(x,x_{1})\geq\frac{7}{16}\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}},

and the following bound holds:

(3.25)

\displaystyle\Re(K_{11}+K_{12})(x,x_{1})\geq\left(\frac{7}{8}-Cw_{0}\right)\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}}.

The same lower bound also holds for
\Re(K_{11}+K_{21})
,
\Re(K_{22}+\tilde{K}_{12})
and
\Re(K_{22}+\tilde{K}_{21})
. Moreover,
D_{11}
is a perturbation of
D_{11}^{0}
:

(3.26)

\displaystyle\left|D_{11}(x,x_{1})-D_{11}^{0}(x,x_{1})\right|\leq Cw_{0}D_{11}^{0}(x,x_{1}).

(iii) Controls for nonlinear terms:

(3.27)

|e_{f}|+|e_{g}|\leq Cw_{0}^{2}(\Delta x)^{-2},

(3.28)

|K_{12}-\tilde{K}_{12}|+|K_{21}-\tilde{K}_{21}|\leq Cw_{0}(\Delta x)^{-2}.

Proof.

(i) Recall that
K_{f}^{0}(x,x_{1})=K_{g}^{0}(x,x_{1})=G_{0}(\Delta x,2\sigma)^{-1}F_{0}(\Delta x,2\sigma)
and

G_{0}(\Delta x,2\sigma)=(\Delta x)^{2}\left[(\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}\right]^{4},

F_{0}(\Delta x,2\sigma)=(2\sigma)^{8}+5(\Delta x)^{2}(2\sigma)^{6}+7(\Delta x)^{4}(2\sigma)^{4}+3(\Delta x)^{6}(2\sigma)^{2}\simeq(2\sigma)^{2}\left[(\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}\right]^{3}.

Hence
D_{22}^{0}(x,x_{1})\simeq\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\frac{(2\sigma)^{2}}{(\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}}.

By virtue of
w_{0}\ll 1
, the following inequalities hold:

(3.29)

\left|\left|(\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta f)^{2}\right|-|\Delta x|^{2}\right|\leq\left|(\Delta f)^{2}\right|\leq w_{0}^{2}(\Delta x)^{2},

(3.30)

\displaystyle\left|\left|(\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta f+2\sigma+\theta(x_{1}))^{2}\right|-\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}\right)\right|\leq

\displaystyle\left|\Delta f+\theta(x_{1})\right|\left|\Delta f+\theta(x_{1})+4\sigma\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle Cw_{0}\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}\right),

(3.31)

\displaystyle\left|\left|(\Delta x)^{2}+(-\Delta f+2\sigma+\theta(x))^{2}\right|-\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}\right)\right|\leq

\displaystyle\left|\Delta f-\theta(x)\right|\left|-\Delta f+\theta(x)+4\sigma\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle Cw_{0}\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}\right),

(3.32)

(\Delta x)^{2}\left|\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta f)^{2}}-\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\right|\leq\left|\frac{(\Delta f)^{2}}{(\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta f)^{2}}\right|\leq\frac{w_{0}^{2}}{1-w_{0}^{2}}\leq Cw_{0}^{2},

(3.33)

\displaystyle\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}\right)\left|\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}}-\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta f+2\sigma+\theta(x_{1}))^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\frac{(\Delta f+4\sigma+\theta(x_{1}))(\Delta f+\theta(x_{1}))}{(\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta f+2\sigma+\theta(x_{1}))^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{Cw_{0}\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}\right)}{(1-Cw_{0})\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle Cw_{0},

and similarly

(3.34)

\displaystyle\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}\right)\left|\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}}-\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}+(-\Delta f+2\sigma+\theta(x))^{2}}\right|\leq Cw_{0}.

Using Lemma
3.5
with inequalities (
3.29
-
3.34
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left|G_{0}(\Delta x,2\sigma)^{-1}-G\left(\Delta x,2\sigma;\frac{\Delta f}{\Delta x},\frac{\theta(x)}{2\sigma},\frac{\theta(x_{1})}{2\sigma}\right)^{-1}\right|\leq Cw_{0}G_{0}(\Delta x,2\sigma)^{-1}.

Recall that

F(\Delta x,2\sigma;w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})=\sum_{j=0}^{3}c_{2j}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3})(\Delta x)^{2j}(2\sigma)^{8-2j}

with
c_{2j}
depending smoothly on
w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}
, and that all coefficients of
F_{0}
are positive. Hence

\left|F\left(\Delta x,2\sigma;\frac{\Delta f}{\Delta x},\frac{\theta(x_{1})}{2\sigma},\frac{\theta(x)}{2\sigma}\right)-F_{0}(\Delta x,2\sigma)\right|\leq Cw_{0}F_{0}(\Delta x,2\sigma).

By Lemma
3.5
again, we obtain

(3.35)

\displaystyle\left|K_{f}(x,x_{1})-K_{f}^{0}(x,x_{1})\right|\leq Cw_{0}K_{f}^{0}(x,x_{1}).

Since
K_{f}^{0}(x,x_{1})
is real-valued, it follows that

(1-Cw_{0})K_{f}^{0}(x,x_{1})\leq\Re K_{f}(x,x_{1})\leq(1+Cw_{0})K_{f}^{0}(x,x_{1}).

By repeating the above arguments, we also have

(1-Cw_{0})K_{g}^{0}(x,x_{1})\leq\Re K_{g}(x,x_{1})\leq(1+Cw_{0})K_{g}^{0}(x,x_{1}),

and thus

\frac{1}{2}-Cw_{0}\leq\frac{\Re K_{f}}{\Re K_{f}+\Re K_{g}}\leq\frac{1}{2}+Cw_{0}.

(ii) Using the inequalities

\frac{1}{8(\Delta x)^{2}}+\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}-(2\sigma)^{2}}{\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}\right)^{2}}=\frac{\left(3\Delta x-2\sigma\right)^{2}}{8(\Delta x)^{2}\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}\right)^{2}}\geq 0,\quad\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}-(2\sigma)^{2}}{\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}\right)^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}},

D_{11}^{0}
can be bounded from below and above:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\geq D_{11}^{0}(x,x_{1})\geq\left(\mu_{1}^{2}+\mu_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\right)\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}}=\left(\frac{7}{16}+\frac{9}{16}(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})^{2}\right)\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}}.

Next, we compute

\displaystyle\left|\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(\Delta f)^{2}-(2\sigma+\theta(x_{1}))^{2}\right)-\left((\Delta x)^{2}-(2\sigma)^{2}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\Delta f|^{2}+|4\sigma+\theta(x_{1})||\theta(x_{1})|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle Cw_{0}\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}\right).

The similar bounds hold with
f
replaced by
g
or
\theta(x_{1})
replaced by
\theta(x)
.
Hence by Lemma
3.5

\displaystyle\left|(K_{11}+K_{12})(x,x_{1})-\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}}-\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}-(2\sigma)^{2}}{\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}\right)^{2}}\right|\leq\frac{Cw_{0}}{(\Delta x)^{2}},

and consequently,

\displaystyle\Re(K_{11}+K_{12})(x,x_{1})\geq\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}}+\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}-(2\sigma)^{2}}{\left((\Delta x)^{2}+(2\sigma)^{2}\right)^{2}}-\frac{Cw_{0}}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\geq\left(\frac{7}{8}-Cw_{0}\right)\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}}.

The lower bounds of
\Re(K_{11}+K_{21})
,
\Re(K_{22}+\tilde{K}_{12})
,
\Re(K_{22}+\tilde{K}_{21})
can be proved in the same way. Moreover, from (
3.24
) it follows

\displaystyle\left|D_{11}(x,x_{1})-D_{11}^{0}(x,x_{1})\right|\leq Cw_{0}\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\leq Cw_{0}D_{11}^{0}(x,x_{1}).

(iii) Using Lemma
3.4
and that
G\left(\Delta x,2\sigma;\frac{\Delta f}{\Delta x},\frac{\theta(x)}{2\sigma},\frac{\theta(x_{1})}{2\sigma}\right)\simeq G_{0}(\Delta x,2\sigma)
, we see that

(3.36)

\displaystyle|e_{f}(x,x_{1})|=

\displaystyle\left|\Delta\theta\Delta f\cdot G\left(\Delta x,2\sigma;\frac{\Delta f}{\Delta x},\frac{\theta(x)}{2\sigma},\frac{\theta(x_{1})}{2\sigma}\right)^{-1}E\left(\Delta x,2\sigma;\frac{\Delta f}{\Delta x},\frac{\theta(x)}{2\sigma},\frac{\theta(x_{1})}{2\sigma}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq
